## General Guidelines

### Guidelines for Using Symbols for Physical Quantities, Chemical Elements and Nuclides

* Symbols for physical quantities are normally single letters and printed in italic (or sloping) type. However, in case of the two letter symbols, appearing as a factor in a product, some spacing is necessary to separate this symbol from other symbols.
* Abbreviations, i.e., shortened forms of names or expressions, such as p.e. for potential energy, are not used in physical equations. These abbreviations in the text are written in ordinary normal/roman (upright) type.
* Vectors are printed in bold and normal/roman (upright) type. However, in class room situations, vectors may be indicated by an arrow on the top of the symbol.
* Multiplication or product of two physical quantities is written with some spacing between them. Division of one physical quantity by another may be indicated with a horizontal bar or with

--- Page 77 ---

## APPENDIX A 8
## GENERAL GUIDELINES FOR USING SYMBOLS FOR SI UNITS, SOME OTHER UNITS, AND SI PREFIXES

* Symbols for units of physical quantities are printed/written in Normal/Roman (upright) type.
* Standard and recommended symbols for units are written in lower case roman (upright) type, starting with small letters. The shorter designations for units such as kg, m, s, cd, etc., are symbols and not the abbreviations. The unit names are never capitalised. However, the unit symbols are capitalised only if the symbol for a unit is derived from a proper name of scientist, beginning with a capital, normal/roman letter.

For example, 'm' for the unit 'metre', 'd' for the unit 'day', 'atm' for the unit 'atmospheric pressure', 'Hz' for the unit 'hertz', 'Wb' for the unit 'weber', 'J' for the unit 'joule', 'A' for the unit 'ampere', 'V' for the unit 'volt', etc. The single exception is L, which is the symbol for the unit 'litre'. This exception is made to avoid confusion of the lower case letter l with the Arabic numeral 1.

* Symbols for units do not contain any final full stop at the end of recommended letter and remain unaltered in the plural, using only singular form of the unit.

For example, for a length of 25 centimetres the unit symbol is written as 25 cm and not 25 cms or 25 cm. or 25 cms., etc.

* Use of solidus ( / ) is recommended only for indicating a division of one letter unit symbol by another unit symbol. Not more than one solidus is used.

For example : 

$\frac{\text{m}}{\text{s}^2}$ or $\text{m s}^{-2}$ (with a spacing between m and $\text{s}^{-2}$) but not $\text{m/s}$; 

$1 \text{ Pl} = 1 \text{ N s m}^{-2} = 1 \text{ N s/m}^2 = 1 \text{ kg/s m} = 1 \text{ kg m}^{-1} \text{ s}^{-1}$, but not $1 \text{ kg/m/s}$; 

$\text{J/K mol}$ or $\text{J K}^{-1} \text{ mol}^{-1}$, but not $\text{J/K/mol}$; etc.

* Prefix symbols are printed in normal/roman (upright) type without spacing between the prefix symbol and the unit symbol. Thus certain approved prefixes written very close to the unit symbol are used to indicate decimal fractions or multiples of a SI unit, when it is inconveniently small or large.

For example :

megawatt ($1 \text{MW} = 10^6 \text{ W}$); 

nanosecond ($1 \text{ ns} = 10^{-9} \text{ s}$); 

centimetre ($1 \text{ cm} = 10^{-2} \text{ m}$); 

picofarad ($1 \text{ pF} = 10^{-12} \text{ F}$);.

kilometre ($1 \text{ km} = 10^3 \text{ m}$); 

microsecond ($1 \mu\text{s} = 10^{-6} \text{ s}$); 

millivolt ($1 \text{ mV}= 10^{-3} \text{ V}$); 

gigahertz ($1 \text{ GHz} = 10^9 \text{ Hz}$);

--- Page 78 ---

## Units and Prefixes in Physics

The unit kilowatt-hour is defined as $1 \text{ kW h} = 10^3 \text{ W h} = 3.6 \text{ MJ} = 3.6 \times 10^6 \text{ J}$. Other units include:
- microampere ($1 \mu \text{A} = 10^{-6} \text{ A}$)
- micron ($1 \mu \text{m} = 10^{-6} \text{ m}$)
- angstrom ($1 \AA = 0.1 \text{ nm} = 10^{-10} \text{ m}$)

The term 'micron', equivalent to $10^{-6} \text{ m}$ or $1 \mu \text{m}$, is a convenient sub-multiple of the metre. Similarly, the unit 'fermi', equal to a femtometre or $10^{-15} \text{ m}$, is used in nuclear studies. The unit 'barn', equal to $10^{-28} \text{ m}^2$, measures cross-sectional areas in sub-atomic particle collisions.

### Rules for Unit Symbols

When using prefixes with unit symbols:
- A prefix and symbol combination is considered a new symbol and can be raised to a power without brackets. For example: 
    - $\text{cm}^3 = (\text{cm})^3 = (10^{-2} \text{ m})^3 = 10^{-6} \text{ m}^3$
    - $\text{mA}^2 = (\text{mA})^2 = (10^{-3} \text{ A})^2 = 10^{-6} \text{ A}^2$

- A prefix is never used alone and must be attached to a unit symbol. For example:
    - $1 \text{ cm}^{-1} = (10^{-2} \text{ m})^{-1} = 10^2 \text{ m}^{-1}$
    - $1 \mu \text{s}^{-1} = (10^{-6} \text{ s})^{-1} = 10^6 \text{ s}^{-1}$

- Prefix symbols are written close to the unit symbol without spacing. Unit symbols are written separately with spacing when units are multiplied. For example:
    - $\text{m} \text{ s}^{-1}$ (symbols $\text{m}$ and $\text{s}$, in lower case) means 'metre per second'
    - $\text{ms}^{-1}$ [symbols $\text{m}$ and $\text{s}$ are written very close to each other] means 'per millisecond'

- The use of double prefixes is avoided when single prefixes are available.

[No diagrams or graphs are present on this page.]

--- Page 79 ---

## Introduction to SI Units and Notation

The following are guidelines for the correct use of SI units, prefixes, and related symbols for physical quantities.

### Unit Definitions

* $10^{-9} \text{ m} = 1 \text{ nm}$ (nanometre)
* $10^{-6} \text{ m} = 1 \mu\text{m}$ (micron)
* $10^{-12} \text{ F} = 1 \text{ pF}$ (picofarad)
* $10^{9} \text{ W} = 1 \text{ GW}$ (giga watt)

### Unit Combinations

The use of a combination of unit and the symbols for units is avoided when the physical quantity is expressed by combining two or more units. For example:
* joule per mole kelvin is written as $\text{J/mol K}$ or $\text{J mol}^{-1} \text{ K}^{-1}$
* joule per tesla is written as $\text{J/T}$ or $\text{J T}^{-1}$
* newton metre second is written as $\text{N m s}$

### Prefix Symbols

To simplify calculations, the prefix symbol is attached to the unit symbol in the numerator and not to the denominator. For example:
* $10^{6} \text{ N/m}^2$ is written more conveniently as $\text{MN/m}^2$

### Physical Quantities and Units

Proper care is needed when same symbols are used for physical quantities and units of physical quantities. For example:
* The physical quantity weight ($W$) expressed as a product of mass ($m$) and acceleration due to gravity ($g$) may be written in terms of symbols $W$, $m$ and $g$ printed in italic (or sloping) type as $W = m g$
* In the equation $W = m g$, the symbol $W$ expresses the weight with a unit symbol $\text{J}$, $m$ as the mass with a unit symbol $\text{kg}$ and $g$ as the acceleration due to gravity with a unit symbol $\text{m/s}^2$

### SI Base Unit

* SI base unit 'kilogram' for mass is formed by attaching SI prefix (a multiple equal to $10^3$) 'kilo' to a cgs (centimetre, gram, second) unit 'gram'
* A thousandth part of unit of length (metre) is called a millimetre (mm)
* A thousandth part of the unit of mass (kg) is not called a millikilogram, but just a gram

### Examples

* $10^3 \text{ kg} = 1 \text{ Mg}$ (megagram)
* $10^{-6} \text{ kg} = 1 \text{ mg}$ (milligram)
* $10^{-3} \text{ kg} = 1 \text{ g}$ (gram)

It may be emphasised again that you should use the internationally approved and recommended symbols only. Continual practice of following general rules and guidelines in unit symbol writing would make you learn mastering the correct use of SI units, prefixes and related symbols for physical quantities in a proper perspective. [No diagrams or graphs are present on this page.]

| 23. | Angular velocity | Angle/time | $[L^0]/[T]$ | $[M^0L^0 T^{-1}]$ |

| 29. | Torque | $\tau = \frac{\Delta L}{\Delta t}$ or $\tau = r \times F$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-1}]}{[\mathrm{T}]}$ or $[\mathrm{MLT}^{-2}][\mathrm{L}]$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | $\mathrm{N} \cdot \mathrm{m}$ |
| 30. | Angular frequency | $\omega = 2 \pi \nu$ | $[\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^0\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | $\mathrm{rad}/\mathrm{s}$ |
| 31. | Wavelength | $\lambda$ | $[\mathrm{L}]$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^1\mathrm{T}^0]$ | m |
| 32. | Hubble constant | $H_0 = \frac{v}{d}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{LT}^{-1}]}{[\mathrm{L}]}$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^0\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | $(\mathrm{km}/\mathrm{s})/\mathrm{Mpc}$ |
| 33. | Intensity of wave | $I = \frac{P}{A}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]/[\mathrm{T}]}{[\mathrm{L}^2]}$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^0\mathrm{T}^{-3}]$ | $\mathrm{W}/\mathrm{m}^2$ |
| 34. | Radiation pressure | $p = \frac{I}{c}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{MT}^{-3}]/[\mathrm{LT}^{-1}]}{}$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^{-1}\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | Pa |
| 35. | Energy density | $u = \frac{E}{V}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]}{[\mathrm{L}^3]}$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^{-1}\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | $\mathrm{J}/\mathrm{m}^3$ |
| 36. | Critical velocity | $v_c = \frac{\mathrm{Re} \cdot \eta}{\rho \cdot r}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^0\mathrm{T}^0][\mathrm{ML}^{-1}\mathrm{T}^{-1}]}{[\mathrm{ML}^{-3}][\mathrm{L}]}$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^1\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | m/s |
| 37. | Escape velocity | $v_e = \sqrt{2gr}$ | $\sqrt{[\mathrm{LT}^{-2}][\mathrm{L}]}$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^1\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | m/s |
| 38. | Heat energy, internal energy | $Q = F \cdot d$ | $[\mathrm{MLT}^{-2}][\mathrm{L}]$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | J |
| 39. | Kinetic energy | $K = \frac{1}{2}mv^2$ | $[\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{LT}^{-1}]^2$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | J |
| 40. | Potential energy | $U = mgh$ | $[\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{LT}^{-2}][\mathrm{L}]$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | J |
| 41. | Rotational kinetic energy | $K_r = \frac{1}{2}I\omega^2$ | $\frac{1}{2} \times [\mathrm{ML}^2] \times [\mathrm{T}^{-1}]^2$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | J |
| 42. | Efficiency | $\eta = \frac{W_\mathrm{out}}{W_\mathrm{in}}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]}{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]}$ | $[\mathrm{M}^0\mathrm{L}^0\mathrm{T}^0]$ | - |
| 43. | Angular impulse | $J = \tau \Delta t$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}][\mathrm{T}]$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | $\mathrm{N} \cdot \mathrm{m} \cdot \mathrm{s}$ |
| 44. | Gravitational constant | $G = \frac{F \cdot r^2}{m_1 m_2}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{MLT}^{-2}][\mathrm{L}]^2}{[\mathrm{M}][\mathrm{M}]}$ | $[\mathrm{M}^{-1}\mathrm{L}^3\mathrm{T}^{-2}]$ | $\mathrm{m}^3/(\mathrm{kg} \cdot \mathrm{s}^2)$ |
| 45. | Planck constant | $h = \frac{E}{\nu}$ | $\frac{[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-2}]}{[\mathrm{T}^{-1}]}$ | $[\mathrm{ML}^2\mathrm{T}^{-1}]$ | $\mathrm{J} \cdot \mathrm{s}$ |

## Physics Constants and Formulas

### Thermal Properties

* **46. Heat capacity, entropy**: $C = \frac{Q}{\Delta T}$ 
* **47. Specific heat capacity**: $c = \frac{Q}{m \Delta T}$ 
* **48. Latent heat**: $L = \frac{Q}{m}$ 
* **49. Thermal expansion coefficient or Thermal expansivity**: $\alpha = \frac{\Delta L}{L_0 \Delta T}$ 
* **50. Thermal conductivity**: $k = \frac{Qd}{At\Delta T}$ 

### Dynamics and Thermodynamics

* **51. Bulk modulus or (compressibility)$^{-1}$**: $B = \frac{V \Delta P}{\Delta V}$ 
* **52. Centripetal acceleration**: $a_c = \frac{v^2}{r}$ 
* **53. Stefan constant**: $\sigma = \frac{E}{AtT^4}$ 
* **54. Wien constant**: $b = \lambda_{max}T$ 
* **55. Boltzmann constant**: $k_B = \frac{E}{T}$ 

### Electromagnetism and Electronics

* **56. Universal gas constant**: $R = \frac{PV}{nT}$ 
* **57. Charge**: $Q = It$ 
* **58. Current density**: $J = \frac{I}{A}$ 
* **59. Voltage, electric potential, electromotive force**: $V = \frac{W}{Q}$ 
* **60. Resistance**: $R = \frac{V}{I}$ 
* **61. Capacitance**: $C = \frac{Q}{V}$ 
* **62. Electrical resistivity or (electrical conductivity)$^{-1}$**: $\rho = \frac{RA}{l}$ 
* **63. Electric field**: $E = \frac{F}{Q}$ 
* **64. Electric flux**: $\Phi_E = EA$ 

[Graphs/Figures: A table with 4 columns and 19 rows, describing various physical quantities, their formulas, and dimensions.]

--- Page 83 ---

## Electric Dipole Moment
The electric dipole moment is given by $\frac{\text{Torque}}{\text{Electric Field}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-2}]}{[MLT^{-3} A^{-1}]} = [M^0 L^3 T A]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Electric Field Strength or Electric Intensity
The electric field strength or electric intensity is given by $\frac{\text{Potential Difference}}{\text{Distance}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-3} A^{-1}]}{[L]} = [MLT^{-3} A^{-1}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Magnetic Field
The magnetic field is given by $\frac{\text{Force}}{\text{Current} \times \text{Length}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[MLT^{-2}]}{[A][L]} = [ML^0 T^{-2} A^{-1}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Magnetic Flux
The magnetic flux is given by $\text{Magnetic Field} \times \text{Area}$. 
Its unit is $[MT^{-2} A^{-1}][L^2] = [ML^2 T^{-2} A^{-1}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Inductance
The inductance is given by $\frac{\text{Magnetic Flux}}{\text{Current}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-2} A^{-1}]}{[A]} = [ML^2 T^{-2} A^{-2}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Magnetic Dipole Moment
The magnetic dipole moment is given by $\frac{\text{Torque}}{\text{Magnetic Field}}$ or $\text{Current} \times \text{Area}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-3}]}{[MT^{-2} A^{-1}]} = [M^0 L^2 T^0 A]$ or $[A][L^2] = [M^0 L^2 T^0 A]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Magnetic Field Strength, Magnetic Intensity or Magnetic Moment Density
The magnetic field strength, magnetic intensity or magnetic moment density is given by $\frac{\text{Magnetic Moment}}{\text{Volume}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[L^2 A]}{[L^3]} = [M^0 L^{-1} T^0 A]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Permittivity Constant (of Free Space)
The permittivity constant (of free space) is given by $\frac{\text{Charge} \times \text{Charge}}{4 \pi \times \text{Electric Force} \times (\text{Distance})^2}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[AT][AT]}{[MLT^{-2}][L^2]} = [M^{-1} L^{-3} T^4 A^2]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Permeability Constant (of Free Space)
The permeability constant (of free space) is given by $\frac{2 \pi \times \text{Force} \times \text{Distance}}{\text{Current} \times \text{Current} \times \text{Length}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[M^0 L^0 T^0][MLT^{-2}][L]}{[A][A][L]} = [MLT^{-2} A^{-2}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Refractive Index
The refractive index is given by $\frac{\text{Speed of Light in Vacuum}}{\text{Speed of Light in Medium}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[LT^{-1}]}{[LT^{-1}]} = [M^0 L^0 T^0]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Faraday Constant
The Faraday constant is given by $\text{Avogadro Constant} \times \text{Elementary Charge}$. 
Its unit is $[AT]/[mol] = [M^0 L^0 T^0 A mol^{-1}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Wave Number
The wave number is given by $\frac{2 \pi}{\text{Wavelength}}$. 
Its unit is $[M^0 L^0 T^0]/[L] = [M^0 L^{-1} T^0]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Radiant Flux, Radiant Power
The radiant flux, radiant power is given by $\frac{\text{Energy Emitted}}{\text{Time}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-2}]}{[T]} = [ML^2 T^{-3}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Luminosity of Radiant Flux or Radiant Intensity
The luminosity of radiant flux or radiant intensity is given by $\frac{\text{Radiant Power or Radiant Flux of Source}}{\text{Solid Angle}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-3}]}{[M^0 L^0 T^0]} = [ML^2 T^{-3}]$.
[No diagram provided]

## Luminous Power or Luminous Flux of Source
The luminous power or luminous flux of source is given by $\frac{\text{Luminous Energy Emitted}}{\text{Time}}$. 
Its unit is $\frac{[ML^2 T^{-2}]}{[T]} = [ML^2 T^{-3}]$.
[No diagram provided]

--- Page 84 ---

## Physics Formulas and Units

### 80. Luminous Intensity or Illuminating Power of Source
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Luminous flux}}{\text{Solid angle}}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]}{[\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]} = [\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]$

### 81. Intensity of Illumination or Luminance
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Luminous intensity}}{(\text{distance})^2}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]/[\text{L}^2]}{[\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]} = [\text{ML}^0\text{T}^{-3}]$

### 82. Relative Luminosity
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Luminous flux of a source of given wavelength}}{\text{luminous flux of peak sensitivity wavelength (555 nm) source of same power}}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]}{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]} = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]$

### 83. Luminous Efficiency
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Total luminous flux}}{\text{Total radiant flux}}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]}{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]} = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]$

### 84. Illuminance or Illumination
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Luminous flux incident}}{\text{area}}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3}]}{[\text{L}^2]} = [\text{ML}^0\text{T}^{-3}]$

### 85. Mass Defect
* Formula: $(\text{sum of masses of nucleons})-(\text{mass of the nucleus})$
* Unit: $[\text{M}] = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]$

### 86. Binding Energy of Nucleus
* Formula: $\text{Mass defect} \times (\text{speed of light})^2$
* Unit: $[\text{M}][\text{L T}^{-1}]^2 = [\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-2}]$

### 87. Decay Constant
* Formula: $\frac{0.693}{\text{half life}}$
* Unit: $[\text{T}^{-1}] = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^{-1}]$

### 88. Resonant Frequency
* Formula: $(\text{Inductance} \times \text{capacitance})^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ 
* Unit: $[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-2} \text{ A}^{-2}]^{-\frac{1}{2}} \times [\text{M}^{-1} \text{ L}^{-2} \text{ T}^4 \text{ A}^2]^{-\frac{1}{2}} = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{A}^0 \text{ T}^{-1}]$

### 89. Quality Factor or Q-Factor of Coil
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Resonant frequency} \times \text{inductance}}{\text{Resistance}}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{T}^{-1}][\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-2} \text{ A}^{-2}]}{[\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3} \text{ A}^{-2}]} = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]$

### 90. Power of Lens
* Formula: $(\text{Focal length})^{-1}$
* Unit: $[\text{L}^{-1}] = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^{-1} \text{ T}^0]$

### 91. Magnification
* Formula: $\frac{\text{Image distance}}{\text{Object distance}}$
* Unit: $[\text{L}]/[\text{L}] = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^0\text{T}^0]$

### 92. Fluid Flow Rate
* Formula: $\frac{(\pi / 8) (\text{pressure}) \times (\text{radius})^4}{(\text{viscosity coefficient}) \times (\text{length})}$
* Unit: $\frac{[\text{ML}^{-1} \text{ T}^{-2}][\text{L}^4]}{[\text{ML}^{-1} \text{ T}^{-1}][\text{L}]} = [\text{M}^0\text{L}^1\text{T}^{-1}]$

### 93. Capacitive Reactance
* Formula: $(\text{Angular frequency} \times \text{capacitance})^{-1}$
* Unit: $[\text{T}^{-1}]^{-1} [\text{M}^{-1} \text{ L}^{-2} \text{ T}^4 \text{ A}^2]^{-1} = [\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^3 \text{ A}^{-2}]$

### 94. Inductive Reactance
* Formula: $(\text{Angular frequency} \times \text{inductance})$
* Unit: $[\text{T}^{-1}][\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-2} \text{ A}^{-2}] = [\text{ML}^2 \text{ T}^{-3} \text{ A}^{-2}]$
